> Looking at the diagnostic output, I'm seeing warnings cluster around two stages: first, during plane-bspline surface construction where five edges between planar and bspline faces are failing to create proper B-spline curves and falling back to mesh-boundary chains, and second during face validation where several of those same faces are flagging BRepCheck failures.
> 
> The model appears to be a calibration cube with engraved or embossed text (X, Y, Z marks), which explains why the fitting process generated six B-spline surfaces across 66 faces — the curved geometry from the text features is being captured by those splines. The core issue seems to be that the intersection surface computation between the planar faces and these fitted B-spline surfaces is returning zero lines, which suggests the surfaces either aren't intersecting properly or the intersection algorithm is struggling with the geometry. Let me look at the source code for the plane-bspline intersection logic and the BRepCheck validation to understand what's happening.

> I'm trying to understand why the plane-bspline intersection is failing with zero lines—this suggests the surfaces aren't actually intersecting where they should be. The cube likely has embossed or engraved text (like "XYZ" markings), so where planar faces meet the freeform bspline surfaces of the text, the intersection calculation is breaking down, which then cascades into BRepCheck failures for those specific faces.
> 
> When the exact intersection curve can't be computed, the code falls back to using a mesh-boundary chain—essentially interpolating vertices from the mesh boundary directly. This approximation only guarantees proximity to the boundary but doesn't lie exactly on both surfaces, so when these chain-based edges are projected onto the surface parameter space as pcurves, they don't form a watertight trimming loop. The edges may not align perfectly with the surface, causing self-intersecting or invalid wires that BRepCheck flags as problematic.
> 
> I should look at how BRepCheck constructs its warning messages to understand the exact failure mode better.

> I'm skipping the formal task tracking since this is a quick diagnostic investigation. Let me examine faces 3, 4, 9, 11 and 49, 50, 52, 53 to see what's happening geometrically—the STL file appears to be a calibration cube with X, Y, Z letters embossed on different faces to test 3D printer axis orientation and alignment.
> 
> With 566 vertices and 1128 faces plus 6 B-spline surfaces covering 66 faces (likely the raised letter geometry on three or six faces), the issue becomes clearer: at the boundary where a planar cube face meets a B-spline letter surface, there's a geometric mismatch. The planar face uses an infinite mathematical plane, while the B-spline surface is a bounded patch fitted only to the letter's geometry, so the parametric domain of the spline doesn't actually extend far enough to intersect with the plane—the fitted surface only covers the letter itself and doesn't reach the plane boundary.
> 
> More likely, embossed letters have a thin vertical wall (a separate planar extrusion) connecting the letter's top to the cube face, so the B-spline captures only the curved/sloped parts while the vertical wall is its own face. This means the B-spline and plane only touch along their boundary curve, and since they're tangent there rather than transversally intersecting, IntSS returns zero intersection lines because the algorithm expects curves that cross, not just touch. The tangency detection finding 8 of 147 edges as tangent aligns with this—these are likely the boundary curves where surfaces meet but don't cross. r a fit that's slightly off or doesn't reach the plane exactly. This aligns with a known OCCT limitation where surface approximation can overshoot tolerance — the raised letters (likely split into multiple faces per character) were fit as B-spline surfaces that don't quite intersect the base plane mathematically, so IntSS correctly finds zero intersection curves because the surfaces genuinely don't cross in 3D space.
> 
> The real issue is that five edges between the planar cube faces and the fitted letter surfaces fail to construct because the two surfaces meet nearly tangentially rather than at a sharp crease, and OCCT's surface-surface intersection numerics can't seed a valid intersection curve in that smooth blending region.
> 
> The code falls back to building a curve directly from the mesh boundary polyline, which succeeds for all five edges with deviation under 5.3e-2mm, so Stage 3.3 reports all 147 curves computed — it's a warning, not an error, since the fallback worked. But downstream, this mesh-boundary approximation doesn't lie exactly on either surface or their parametric curves, which creates a downstream problem. The fallback chain-based edge curve causes BRepCheck failures because the projected pcurve doesn't align properly with the bspline surface trim, likely creating self-intersections in parameter space—especially for face 3's "intersecting wires" error. I'm investigating whether the pcurve projection to the bspline surface has special handling or if it fails because the 3D chain curve doesn't actually lie on the surface geometry.

```
2188	    p0: &gp::Pnt,
2189	    p1: &gp::Pnt,
2190	    p2: &gp::Pnt,
2191	) -> Option<OwnedPtr<geom::HandleGeomCurve>> {
2192	    // Vectors from p0 to p1 and p2
2193	    let a = [p1.x() - p0.x(), p1.y() - p0.y(), p1.z() - p0.z()];
2194	    let b = [p2.x() - p0.x(), p2.y() - p0.y(), p2.z() - p0.z()];
2195	
2196	    // Normal = cross(a, b)
2197	    let n = [
2198	        a[1] * b[2] - a[2] * b[1],
2199	        a[2] * b[0] - a[0] * b[2],
2200	        a[0] * b[1] - a[1] * b[0],
2201	    ];
2202	    let n2 = n[0] * n[0] + n[1] * n[1] + n[2] * n[2];
```

## 🤖💭 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd59L2MGYobjxdgBQpNF3 -->

> So the fallback approach is working as intended—when a straight-line chord deviates too much from the actual boundary chain, it switches to fitting either a circular arc or B-spline through the vertices, which brings the deviation down to acceptable levels in the 1-2 centimeter range. This gives a reasonably accurate geometric representation of the curved mesh boundary. But there's a critical issue emerging that I need to think through.
> 
> The problem is that this fitted 3D curve, while close to the mesh boundary polyline, isn't actually constrained to lie on either the planar surface or the fitted B-spline surface itself. It's just a curve through the 3D vertices. When Stage 3.4 tries to project this curve onto each surface to create the 2D parametric curves needed for trimming, the projection can fail or produce self-intersecting loops if the original 3D curve doesn't sit precisely on the surfaces—which is likely given fabrication noise and the fact that the B-spline surface was fit independently with its own tolerance. This mismatch between the 3D boundary curve and the actual surfaces is what's causing the "intersecting wires" and "invalid wire" errors I'm seeing. ompletely, leaving a gap where the mesh boundary should snap to both surfaces simultaneously. The fallback edge curve bridges this gap by approximating the mesh vertices, but it doesn't actually lie on either surface with the precision OCCT requires, so when Stage 3.4 tries to build trimmed faces using these edges, the 2D parameter-space curves become inconsistent or self-intersecting. This cascades into invalid-wire and unorientable-face errors across the 8 faces sharing those 5 problem edges, which then fails the solid's BRepCheck validation.
> 
> I need to verify this by examining the actual mesh geometry at the affected faces and checking the curve deviations more carefully, but the core issue is clear: the B-spline fit doesn't meet the plane with enough geometric precision, and the fallback recovery strategy sacrifices surface-conformance for mesh-fidelity, breaking STEP validity.
> 
> The real question is whether the final output is still usable in practice — I should run a volume comparison check to see if the geometry remains functionally sound despite the BRepCheck failures.

```
/// Compute maximum distance between STL mesh vertices/centroids and STEP model surfaces,
/// and maximum angular deviation between adjacent STL faces on the same STEP surface.
///
/// Reads an STL file to extract vertex positions and triangle centroids, reads a
/// STEP file to extract surfaces, and computes the minimum distance from each
/// point to any STEP surface. Also builds triangle adjacency and maps each
/// triangle to its nearest STEP surface, then measures the dihedral angle
/// between adjacent triangles sharing the same STEP surface.
///
/// Units: all distances are in mm. OCCT's STEPControl_Reader converts STEP
/// file units (typically meters for Onshape exports) to mm internally.
/// STL files have no unit metadata; coordinates are assumed to be in mm.
use brepper::stage1::{self, VertexWeldOptions};
use opencascade_sys::{
    b_rep, b_rep_builder_api, b_rep_extrema, b_rep_g_prop, extrema, g_prop, gp, message, rw_stl,
    step_control, top_abs, top_exp, topo_ds,
};
use std::collections::HashMap;
use std::env;
use std::process;

fn main() {
    let args: Vec<String> = env::args().collect();

    // Parse --quad flag
    let mut positional = Vec::new();
    let mut use_quad = false;
    for arg in &args[1..] {
        if arg == "--quad" {
            use_quad = true;
        } else {
            positional.push(arg.as_str());
        }
    }
    if positional.len() != 2 {
        eprintln!("Usage: {} [--quad] <input.stl> <input.step>", args[0]);
        process::exit(1);
    }
    let stl_path = positional[0];
    let step_path = positional[1];

    // Read STL → Poly_Triangulation
    if !std::path::Path::new(stl_path).exists() {
        eprintln!("Error: STL file not found: {stl_path}");
        process::exit(1);
    }
    let progress = message::ProgressRange::new();
    let tri_handle = rw_stl::read_file_charptr_progressrange_2(stl_path, &progress);
    let tri = tri_handle.get();
    let num_nodes = tri.nb_nodes();
---

gen_test_models.rs
step_measure_tolerance.rs
viewer.rs
```

> The BRepCheck validation reveals 8 problematic faces out of 54 (faces 3, 4, 9, 11 with intersecting wires, plus B-spline faces 49, 50, 52, 53), all concentrated around the raised letter features. While the geometric deviations are small—worst vertex deviation 0.036mm and centroid max 0.335mm, both well under 1% relative to the 20mm max dimension—the topology is fundamentally compromised at these letter boundaries, meaning the B-rep isn't watertight and some CAD tools may reject or display cracks in the STEP file.

> The root cause traces back to 5 edges where the fitted B-spline letter surfaces meet planar cube faces: the analytic surface-surface intersection fails to find a crossing, so the fallback mesh-boundary-chain curve (accurate to 0.016–0.053mm on the raw mesh) doesn't lie on either adjacent surface. When the trimming and parametric curve stage tries to reconcile this curve with both the plane and B-spline, the resulting wires become geometrically inconsistent with their surfaces, triggering the BRepCheck failures and cascading invalidity through the shell and solid checks.

> Face 3 appears to be a small planar triangle positioned at z=19.5 with a normal vector of [0.826, -0.564, 0], which means it's a slanted facet rather than aligned with the cube's standard top or side faces.
> 
> This is puzzling because multiple faces (3, 4, 9, 11) all share the same z=19.5 centroid with normals that have zero z-component, suggesting they're vertical wall segments rather than horizontal surfaces. Given that the cube is 20×20×20 and these faces cluster near z=19.5 (close to the top at z=20), they're likely the vertical walls of an engraved or embossed letter on the top surface — the side walls of the raised/recessed text that connect different height levels on the top face. So the vertical wall segments form the outline of the letter X, and those B-spline faces are capturing the rounded/filleted edges where the walls meet the curved top surface of the raised letter. The issue is at these boundaries where the planar wall faces meet the smooth B-spline surfaces.
> 
> The intersection solver is failing because those small planar triangles are essentially degenerate remnants from the mesh quantization—they're nearly tangent to the B-spline patches that already cover the fillet region. The fitting process left these tiny slivers unfitted because they were too small or fragmented to gather into a proper B-spline surface, which explains why the intersection calculation can't find a valid line where the plane meets the curve. ident wires), which cascades up through the shell and solid validation. The root cause is clear: those tiny planar slivers are geometrically incompatible with the projection-based trimming workflow when the pcurve errors become significant relative to the face size.
> 
> I've confirmed this with the area discrepancy—the STEP model shows about 9% more area than the STL, which tracks with self-intersecting wire geometry inflating the apparent surface area while volume remains stable. I also verified the "32 below 4 faces" notation from the fitting stage, which explicitly states those regions weren't processed because they fell below the minimum face threshold.
> 
> Now I should write up a clear diagnosis for the user with specific code references and line numbers, explaining the two-stage warning pattern. I'll note that while the deviations are likely benign and the volume is close, the 8 faces are topologically invalid according to BRepCheck, and I should suggest actionable fixes like adjusting the face threshold or extending the absorption logic to capture these slivers into adjacent B-spline patches.
> 
> Let me check the development plan to see if this exact scenario—tiny planar slivers adjacent to B-spline patches failing IntSS—is already documented as a known issue.

```
LEARNINGS.md:212:- \`GeomAPI_IntSS::new_handlegeomsurface2_real(S1, S2, Tol)\` computes surface-surface intersection curves. Returns 1-indexed curves via \`.nb_lines()\` and \`.line(i)\` which returns \`&HandleGeomCurve\`.
LEARNINGS.md:213:- When IntSS returns multiple curves, select the one closest to mesh boundary vertices by projecting sampled boundary points onto each curve via \`GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnCurve\` and picking minimum total distance.
LEARNINGS.md:218:- All current non-tangent edges compute 100% of edge curves successfully via IntSS (cube 12/12, cylinder 2/2, hemisphere 1/1, ball_on_cylinder 2/2, block_with_hole 15/15, pipe 4/4, spherical_pocket 13/13, chamfered_cube 48/48, part_rounded_cube 16/16).
LEARNINGS.md:219:- **Tangent edge curves**: \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` fails for tangent surfaces (returns no curves). Instead, construct tangent edge curves analytically. For plane-cylinder tangencies: compute the radial direction from the component of the plane normal perpendicular to the cylinder axis, find the tangent point at \`axis_origin + r * radial_unit\`, construct a \`Geom_Line\` through that point along the cylinder axis direction, and trim to the vertex endpoint parameters. Validate that boundary vertices lie within tolerance of the constructed curve.
LEARNINGS.md:222:- **Adaptive vertex tolerance for MakeEdge**: When surface fits are imprecise (e.g., cylinders fitted from limited mesh data), IntSS intersection curves may not pass exactly through mesh vertex positions. Before creating MakeEdge, compute the distance from each vertex to the curve at its assigned parameter, and update the OCCT vertex tolerance to at least that distance (with 1% margin). This replaces the fixed \`vertex_tolerance_mm\` (1e-5mm) with position-adaptive tolerances that accommodate surface fit imprecision.
LEARNINGS.md:242:- **Plane-cone and cylinder-cone junctions are never tangent**: At a plane-cone junction (plane ⊥ axis at base), the normals differ by the cone half-angle. At a cylinder-cone junction (coaxial), the cone normal has an axial tilt of sin(half_angle) that the cylinder normal lacks. For any real cone (half-angle > 2°), these exceed the tangency threshold. No test model has cone tangent edges; all cone models pass via the non-tangent IntSS path.
LEARNINGS.md:246:- **The mesh boundary chain is the ground truth for every edge curve** (\`repair_edge_curve_from_boundary\`): after trimming, the curve is validated against a spread of the chain's vertices; if it strays beyond \`--surface-tolerance\`, the curve is rebuilt as the circle through three spread chain vertices and re-trimmed with a chain-supported arc. This one guard catches the complementary-arc trap, degenerate IntSS output with odd parameterization (\`cone_tangent_planes\`' near-tangent coaxial cones: IntSS gave a curve 14 mm off the junction), and straight-line stand-ins for curved junctions — uniformly for any surface pair, including future NURBS.
LEARNINGS.md:247:- **The chain also serves as the last-resort constructor** (\`edge_curve_from_boundary_chain\`): when IntSS/tangent construction fails outright, the edge gets the chain's line-or-circle curve if it fits the chain within \`--surface-tolerance\`; a junction of two *intersecting* fillet cylinders isn't exactly circular, so expect honest residuals (~0.17 mm on stepped_block_toroidal fine) rather than perfection.
LEARNINGS.md:249:- **IntSS degenerate curves for plane-through-cylinder-axis**: When a plane passes through a cylinder's axis, \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` returns 4 degenerate curves with extreme parameter ranges (~-846M to -846M+47k), none containing the model's actual z-range. \`ProjectPointOnCurve\` fails on 3 of 4 curves. Fallback: validate IntSS curve by projecting boundary vertices — if distance exceeds 1mm, construct a \`Geom_Line\` from the two vertex positions instead (the boundary-chain repair then replaces the line when the junction is actually curved).
LEARNINGS.md:250:- **\`is_periodic()\` returns false for IntSS circles**: Circle curves from cylinder×plane⊥axis intersections have \`is_periodic()=false\` despite being full circles with \`first_param=0, last_param=2π\`. Detect by checking \`(last_param - first_param - 2π).abs() < 1e-6\` instead.
LEARNINGS.md:252:- **Centroid-based curve selection**: When IntSS returns multiple curves, selecting the one closest to a centroid computed from boundary vertices is more robust than sequential \`ProjectPointOnCurve\` calls, which can fail on degenerate curves.
LEARNINGS.md:253:- **IntSS partial arcs for sphere-plane through poles**: When a sphere-plane intersection circle passes through the sphere's UV poles (i.e., the plane contains the sphere axis), \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` returns TWO semicircular arcs instead of one full circle. This happens because in UV space, the circle maps to two disconnected meridional lines (U=0 and U=π), which IntSS treats as separate intersection curves. Fix: for closed-loop edges, detect when the curve span < 2π and reconstruct the full circle from 3 sampled points using the circumscribed circle formula (circumcenter from 3 non-collinear points).
LEARNINGS.md:354:4. **What these checks cannot catch**: a *single row* of quads (two rings of vertices) lies EXACTLY on some cone; a *band of azimuthal strips* on a shallow cone lies almost exactly on a tilted cylinder (any 3 plane normals are coplanar-ish). Such spurious hypotheses are geometrically legitimate and unavoidable at the hypothesis level; they are removed by area-based selection plus the post-selection absorption pass (see below). Tests must therefore assert on *selected* surfaces (stage 2.6), never raw hypothesis counts.
LEARNINGS.md:362:Both cone and cylinder LM refinement **recenter** all vertex positions around their centroid before optimization, then shift fitted parameters (apex, axis origin) back afterward. This improves numerical stability when vertex coordinates are large relative to the surface feature size. LM patience is set to 500 (up from default ~100) to ensure convergence well below vertex tolerance. \`seed_vtol_multiplier\` is 1.0 for cones/cylinders since CAD mesh vertices lie precisely on the surface.
LEARNINGS.md:391:The torus fitting algorithm: (1) estimate minor radius r from normal-line intersections of sampled face pairs using **25th percentile** (not median — see below), (2) compute tube centers k_i = p_i + r*n_i (trying both signs, keeping best circle-fit RMS), (3) fit 3D circle to tube centers using PCA for axis + 2D algebraic circle fit for R, (4) refine all 7 parameters [alpha, beta, cx, cy, cz, R, r] via Levenberg-Marquardt (patience=500, threshold=7). For better normal estimates, vertex normals are averaged across all incident seed faces rather than using a single face normal. Vertex iteration is sorted for determinism (HashSet ordering varies between runs).
LEARNINGS.md:456:- Tangent-junction bridge facets (≤ 2 faces): tolerance = surface tolerance. A facet spanning a tangent junction (e.g. cone-sphere on the nosecone) has vertices on both sides; its chordal deviation from either surface is ~d²/2R, far above vertex tolerance but below surface tolerance. Without absorption these become orphan single-face planar surfaces with fake edges that break stage 3.1 compare.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:53:- \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` — Compute intersection curves between two surfaces
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:173:Stages 2.1–2.5 share a common algorithm structure for deducing surface hypotheses from the mesh. This section describes the generic algorithm; each stage section below describes how it is parameterized. The Rust implementation uses the \`SurfaceType\` trait in \`bfs.rs\` to share code across surface types.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:289:**Surface fit**: Attempts both a cone fit (5-parameter) and a cylinder fit (4-parameter) independently, then selects whichever achieves the lower maximum vertex-to-surface distance. If the cone's fitted half-angle is outside the valid range (2°–85°) or its apex is too far from the mesh, the cone fit is rejected. The fits use: (1) Axis direction from smallest eigenvector of the area-weighted normal covariance matrix $M = \sum w_i n_i n_i^T$. (2) For cylinders: project vertices perpendicular to the axis, fit a 2D circle for axis position and radius. For cones: estimate half-angle and apex from vertex positions along the axis. (3) LM refinement minimizing vertex-to-surface distances, with vertices **recentered** around their centroid for numerical stability (parameters shifted back after fitting), and \`patience=500\` to ensure convergence well below vertex tolerance. With \`--verbose 4\` or higher, each seed fit logs both the cone and cylinder fit attempts along with their max vertex/surface errors and rejection reasons. See [SURFACE_FITTING.md](SURFACE_FITTING.md) for detailed fitting math.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:417:2. **Tangent-plane merge**: a multi-face planar selection whose *entire* shared boundary with a component is smooth is the flat part of the same freeform surface (B-spline local support makes exactly-planar margins — bspline_blister) and is merged in; keeping it separate invents a feature edge the reference doesn't have. Real planar neighbors (walls, bottoms) always meet freeform regions across at least some sharp edges. Analytic models are untouched — they have no ≥ 4-face freeform components.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:418:3. Components below 4 faces stay single-face planar. Components planar to fitting exactness (10× vertex tolerance, NaN-guarded: the area-weighted normal of a closed region cancels to zero) become a multi-face planar hypothesis instead — a plane is preferable to a B-spline that overfits noise.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:420:5. **Resample a grid** through the flattened triangulation (uniform-bin point location; numeric misses near corner slivers are clamped to the nearest triangle within one bin — feeding them to the hole membrane extrapolates garbage). Grid stations per axis are the boundary vertices' own pinned positions (stations *between* the vertices of a coarse profile sample chord interiors and pin the fit to the chordal prism — a 1.7% volume deficit on the arch, the mesh's own chordal error), evenly subdivided up to an interior-density target of ~1.5·√(region vertices) (a flat margin tessellates coarsely: hill_with_hole has 8-vertex sides on a 946-vertex region). Genuine hole interiors are filled with a Laplacian membrane so the fitter sees smooth data. The grid is flipped if needed so d1u×d1v matches the mesh's outward normals: B-spline faces are always \`concave = false\`.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:427:**Periodic-band junctions (stage 3.3)**: the band's two cap junctions are closed-loop edges (no vertices) and must carry PERIODIC curves — seam planning re-anchors a closed edge with \`MakeEdge\` over [t, t+period] at a solved seam azimuth, which a closed-but-non-periodic curve refuses. Two mechanisms deliver that (both were needed on elliptical_cylinder): (a) an IntSS curve that comes back closed-but-non-periodic (observed type: \`Geom_TrimmedCurve\`, exact to ~1e-14) is converted via \`GeomConvert::CurveToBSplineCurve\` + \`SetPeriodic\` (exact, measured); (b) an IntSS curve that comes back as a genuine partial arc falls to the boundary-chain repair, whose closed-chain arm interpolates a periodic C2 B-spline through the chain vertices (\`GeomAPI_Interpolate\` with the periodic flag, closing point not repeated — ~4e-15 chain deviation observed). Partial-arc detection for closed loops keys on the ENDPOINT GAP, not the parameter span: a closed B-spline junction arrives over an arbitrary range (observed [0,1]), and the old span≠2π test replaced the true junction with a bogus circle (14.8 mm off, 12% volume error).
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:444:- *Edge curves will lean on the boundary chain.* Expect \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` to be flakier against B-spline surfaces than between quadrics, and expect tangent-classified junctions (a fitted patch usually meets its neighbors near-tangentially) to have no analytic construction — both routes end at the mesh-boundary-chain machinery (\`repair_edge_curve_from_boundary\`, \`edge_curve_from_boundary_chain\`), which is surface-pair-agnostic. The B-spline third arm is implemented: the repair now fits line, circle, AND a \`GeomAPI_PointsToBSpline\` curve through the chain vertices (open chains), keeping whichever deviates least from the chain. The first model to demand it was elliptical_arch — one of its two arch∩wall ellipse junctions came back from IntSS degenerate, and the circle repair's 1 mm deviation cost 1.6% of the solid volume; the chain's B-spline curve fits it to 1.8e-2 mm.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:516:For each ReconEdge, determine whether the two adjacent surfaces are tangent along the shared boundary. This matters because \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` can fail or produce degenerate results for tangent/near-tangent surfaces, requiring special handling in edge curve computation (3.3).
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:543:- For non-tangent edges: use \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` to compute intersection curves with tolerance \`vertex_tolerance_mm\`. IntSS may return multiple curves (e.g., a plane cutting through a torus); \`select_closest_curve()\` picks the one closest to the mesh boundary vertices by sampling up to 10 evenly-spaced boundary vertices, projecting each onto each candidate curve via \`GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnCurve\`, and summing distances.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:544:- For tangent edges: construct the curve analytically rather than using \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` (which fails or produces degenerate results for tangent/near-tangent surfaces). Dispatches by surface pair type:
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:550:  - **Cylinder-cone** (TODO): \`Geom_Circle\` at the axial position where the cone radius equals the cylinder radius (when coaxial), or \`IntSS\`-based (non-coaxial).
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:563:- **Partial arc reconstruction**: When \`IntSS\` returns a partial arc for a closed-loop edge (e.g., a semicircle when a sphere-plane intersection passes through the sphere's UV poles), reconstruct the full circle from 3 sampled points on the arc using the circumscribed circle formula. This fixes cases where the intersection plane contains the sphere axis, causing \`IntSS\` to split the circle into two separate arcs in UV space.
```

> Looking at the documentation, this behavior is explicitly documented as expected — the plane-bspline intersection issues stem from known limitations in how the geometry API handles B-spline surfaces compared to simpler quadric surfaces, and the tangent-classified junctions at mesh boundaries don't have analytic solutions, which is why they fall back to the mesh machinery.
> 
> But the subsequent BRepCheck face-invalid warnings are a different concern — those aren't mentioned in the documentation as expected or tolerated behavior, so I need to search more thoroughly through the development notes to see if there's any mention of BRepCheck failures being acceptable for near-tangent B-spline boundaries, or if this represents an actual unhandled bug or limitation.

```
DONE.md:64:Created OCCT faces from surfaces bounded by edge wires. Handles planar faces with wire-based construction, cylindrical/spherical faces with UV-bounds or wire-based construction, conical and toroidal faces. Validates all faces with \`BRepCheck_Analyzer\`.
DONE.md:70:Constructed solids from shells using \`ShapeFix_Solid::SolidFromShell\`. Validates with \`BRepCheck_Analyzer\`, computes volume via \`BRepGProp::VolumeProperties\`.
LEARNINGS.md:65:- \`BRepCheck\`, \`BRepGProp\`, \`BRepLib\` (no globals)
LEARNINGS.md:233:- **Edge tolerance must cover the pcurve deviation INCLUDING endpoints**: \`BRepCheck_Analyzer::IsValid(face)\` is \`ValidSub\` over the face's wires/edges/vertices — subshape statuses *in the face's context* (see BRepCheck_Analyzer.cxx), notably \`InvalidCurveOnSurface\` which compares pcurve-mapped points against the 3D curve over the whole range against the edge tolerance. Measuring deviation only at interval midpoints under-tolerates the ends and produces "face shape invalid" with no failing sub-status visible.
LEARNINGS.md:236:- \`BRepCheck_Analyzer::new_shape_bool(shape, true)\` validates faces. \`.is_valid()\` returns bool.
LEARNINGS.md:239:- **BRepCheck_Face diagnostics**: \`b_rep_check::Face::new_face(face)\` creates a face checker. Call \`.minimum()\` first to compute all checks, then query \`.intersect_wires(false)\`, \`.classify_wires(false)\`, \`.orientation_of_wires(false)\` (each returns \`b_rep_check::Status\`), and \`.is_unorientable()\` (bool). When a face fails with no sub-status, dump its structure with a plain \`TopExp_Explorer\` over wire edges — \`BRepTools_WireExplorer\` stops silently at the first connectivity break, which is itself a useful signal.
LEARNINGS.md:283:- **Great circle arc fallback for sphere edges**: When \`compute_edge_curve\` finds a large gap (>0.3mm) and one surface is a sphere, constructing a great circle arc on the sphere between the two vertices produces a better edge curve than a straight line. Uses \`geom::Circle\` with proper normal (calculated from sphere center and edge vertices) and \`select_arc_parameters\` to trim. This fixed BRepCheck wire self-intersection failures on sphere faces.
LEARNINGS.md:325:Calling \`BRepLib::SameParameter(shape, 1.0, true)\` with a large tolerance (1.0mm) and \`forced=true\` recomputes all pcurves, which can corrupt pcurves on faces with closely-spaced edges (e.g., sphere faces at sphere-cylinder tangent junctions). This manifests as BRepCheck_Wire self-intersection failures. The fix is to use \`forced=false\` (skip edges already flagged as SameParameter) and a tight tolerance (\`vertex_tolerance_mm\`, typically 1e-5). This allows edges that were correctly constructed to keep their pcurves while only fixing genuinely misaligned ones.
LEARNINGS.md:476:### OCCT detailed BRepCheck diagnostics can throw
LEARNINGS.md:478:\`BRepCheck_Face\`/\`BRepCheck_Edge\` diagnostics on invalid faces can throw \`Standard_NullObject\` (edges without geometry), which the bindings surface as a Rust panic. \`validate_faces\` wraps the detailed diagnostics in \`catch_unwind\` and treats a diagnostic failure as an ordinary invalid face rather than aborting the pipeline.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:76:- \`BRepCheck_Analyzer\` — Validate B-Rep topology/geometry
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:608:- **Pcurves** (\`build_pcurve\`): for every (edge, face) incidence, UV samples of the edge's stored curve are interpolated (\`Geom2dAPI_Interpolate\`) with the curve's own parameters, so SameParameter holds by construction. Endpoint samples snap to the shared vertex's analytic UV (branch-lifted), making wires closed in 2D at their vertices even when a mesh vertex sits slightly off this face's fitted surface. Seam pcurves pin the wrap coordinate to the window borders exactly. Sampling densifies (33→257 points) until the pcurve reproduces the 3D curve within \`vertex_tolerance/10\` or the deviation plateaus (a 3D curve off the fitted surface by its fit residual cannot be represented away); the achieved deviation — measured at samples *and* midpoints, *including endpoints* — sets the edge tolerance (OCCT's in-context BRepCheck validates pcurve/curve agreement over the whole range against it). The pcurves are recorded in \`ReconEdge.pcurves\` and vertex UVs in \`BRepVertex.uv_coords\`.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:614:- Validate each face with \`BRepCheck_Analyzer\`; invalid faces get detailed \`BRepCheck_Face\` diagnostics plus a structural dump (wire order, orientations, pcurve endpoints, vertex parameters) under \`-v\`. Note \`BRepCheck_Analyzer::IsValid(face)\` reflects subshape statuses *in the face's context* (e.g. \`InvalidCurveOnSurface\`), which is why the edge tolerance accounting above matters.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:636:- Call \`BRepLib::SameParameter\` with \`forced=false\` and tight tolerance (\`vertex_tolerance_mm\`) to ensure pcurve/3D curve agreement without corrupting already-correct pcurves. Using \`forced=true\` or a large tolerance (e.g. 1.0mm) recomputes all pcurves and can corrupt faces with closely-spaced tangent edges (e.g. sphere faces at sphere-cylinder junctions), causing BRepCheck self-intersection failures.
DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:638:- Validate with \`BRepCheck_Analyzer\`. Log warning if validation fails (may indicate upstream face issues from stage 3.4).
```
</details>

## Diagnosis

The warnings all trace back to **5 edges where a fitted B-spline patch meets a tiny planar sliver face**, at the raised/engraved letter geometry on this test cube. Two independent tools ran on `tests/downloaded/xyz-cube-test-20mm.stl`: the `brepper` conversion itself, and `step-measure-tolerance` on the resulting STEP, to check whether the warnings represent a real geometric defect or just a validity nag.

**Root cause (Stage 3.2/3.3).** `GeomAPI_IntSS` returns zero intersection lines for 5 plane↔bspline pairs (edges 48, 68, 84, 86, 109 → faces 3/4/9/11 planar vs. 49/50/52/53 bspline), at [stage3.rs:1349](src/stage3.rs:1349). This is a *known, expected* failure mode, already documented at [DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:444](DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:444): a fitted freeform patch usually meets its planar neighbor near-tangentially, and `IntSS` can't seed a transversal intersection walk on a near-tangent pair. The pattern here fits: faces 3/4/9/11 are tiny single-triangle "leftover" planar slivers (stage 2.7's own log says "32 below 4 faces" — too small to be grouped or fit with a surface), sitting right at the edge of the fitted letter surfaces.

**First fallback (works fine).** [`edge_curve_from_boundary_chain`](src/stage3.rs:2063) → [`repair_edge_curve_from_boundary`](src/stage3.rs:1891) rebuilds each of these 5 edges directly from the raw mesh polyline (best of line/circle/C2 B-spline fit), landing 1.6e-2–5.3e-2 mm from the true mesh boundary — good enough that Stage 3.3 reports 147/147 curves computed, no failures. But by construction this curve is *mesh-truth*, not *surface-truth*: it isn't constrained to lie exactly on either the plane or the bspline patch.

**Second-order failure (Stage 3.4).** When that off-surface curve gets trimmed into pcurves for both adjacent faces, the mismatch — negligible on a normal-sized face — is large relative to these sub-mm sliver triangles, producing self-intersecting/invalid wires and an unorientable face on exactly the 8 faces touching those 5 edges. That invalidity then propagates into the shell/solid check ("solid 0 failed BRepCheck").

One extra detail worth noting: face 9 and face 49 each appear in *two* of the five bad edges — they're a pinch point where two bspline patches and a sliver converge (likely where two strokes of a letter meet, e.g. an "X" crossing), which is why they're doubly implicated.

**Practical severity.** I converted the file and cross-checked geometry:
- Volume: STEP 7657.0 mm³ vs. STL 7682.4 mm³ → 0.33% off, well inside the 1% gate.
- Vertex/centroid deviation: max 0.036 mm / 0.335 mm on a 20mm cube.
- Area: STEP 2696.0 mm² vs. STL 2471.7 mm² — **~9% high**, consistent with those invalid/self-intersecting slivers double-covering small patches of surface at the letter edges. This is the one number that shows the defect isn't purely cosmetic.

So: the file is still readable and volumetrically close, but 8 of 54 faces are topologically invalid B-rep at the letter-engraving boundaries — a strict downstream CAD validator would flag/reject those faces even though a mesh-volume comparison wouldn't catch it. This isn't a regression from recent work; it's an un-hardened edge of the documented "tangent junction → boundary-chain fallback" path when the adjacent face is a near-degenerate sliver.

> I found it — a PCURVE with a planar surface at x=9, defined by a B-spline curve with degree 3 and an enormous number of control points (around 260) and corresponding knots.
> 
> The knots are nearly uniformly spaced with multiplicity 1 except at the endpoints (multiplicity 4), creating an extremely fine, densified representation that's almost piecewise linear. The spacing follows a pattern of 1/256 increments, making this curve essentially a heavily oversampled approximation. This PCURVE maps into the plane's 2D (u,v) parameter space, and one of its control points is that wild 2D coordinate (-62.336, 31.855) I spotted earlier.
> 
> This is almost certainly an edge on a small planar sliver face at x=9 — likely one of those leftover slivers from the letter engraving at the base — and the PCURVE represents how that edge curves through the plane's parameter space.
> 
> Now I'm checking whether that extreme 2D point actually maps to valid 3D geometry. Since planes are unbounded and their parameterization is just an affine transformation, any (u,v) values are technically valid and will map to some point on the plane at x=9. The real question is how the AXIS2_PLACEMENT_3D of the plane (#13652) is oriented — I need to figure out which direction is the normal, which is the u-axis, and which is the v-axis to understand where that point actually lands in 3D space. So this wildly out-of-bounds 3D point confirms the geometry issue the user is seeing—the pcurve mapping is using parameter values that are orders of magnitude off, placing points ~60mm away on a 20mm model. This suggests the `build_pcurve` function is constructing the curve with severely incorrect (u,v) control point values rather than just minor errors.
> 
> The interpolation process densifies UV samples from 33 points up to 257 points, trying to reproduce the 3D curve within tolerance, but the fact that it hit maximum densification (257 points) and still couldn't converge means the deviation plateaued—the projection fundamentally can't be made to fit smoothly, suggesting something is deeply wrong with how the 3D curve is being projected onto the plane's UV space.
> 
> One or more of those 257 interpolation samples likely computed a drastically incorrect UV value, probably from a bad local solution in the projection algorithm (like Newton iteration getting stuck), which would create a huge spike in the control polygon that the interpolator tries to smooth over, causing overshoot and oscillation. The root issue appears to be that projecting a 3D point onto the plane's (u,v) parameters produced a wildly wrong UV coordinate for one or a few samples—possibly because those points are numerically degenerate with respect to this particular plane.
> 
> But actually, for a plane, UV projection is just an orthogonal dot product—a linear, continuous, well-conditioned operation with no singularities. A tiny 3D offset can't produce a 60mm UV error unless the 3D point itself is 60mm off. So the underlying 3D curve that both faces reference must already have some corrupted sample point baked into it.
> 
> This points to a failure in the edge curve construction itself—likely a runaway circle-fit or repair operation where three nearly-collinear points produced a huge-radius circle, and when trimmed to the arc parameters, one interior sample ended up wildly displaced.
> 
> I should trace back to find the actual EDGE_CURVE entity for this problematic pcurve and check if it's a Geom_Circle with an unusually large radius, which would confirm the near-collinear repair hypothesis.

This should logically fall somewhere between the surrounding points (around y ≈ 1.8, z ≈ 13.5), but instead it's been flung off with sign-flipped and incorrectly scaled values, suggesting a computational error like a failed Newton iteration or sign corruption.
> 
> This looks like a least-squares B-spline fit that diverged — possibly from a near-singular linear system caused by duplicate or nearly-duplicate input points, which would cause the solver to produce a huge spurious coefficient for one control point. The curve here has 12 poles and degree 3, which might come from the B-spline repair candidate using a point-to-B-spline fit with degree 3 and up to 8 knots, though I'm not entirely sure if this is the exact fit being applied or if it came from an earlier stage.

The surface itself was fit via B-spline surface interpolation over a 10×8 grid. The fit surface passes through its resampled grid points with essentially zero error, but that doesn't rule out wild oscillations in the control points between those grid points—a classic Runge phenomenon issue that can occur with non-uniform data spacing or ill-conditioned input. The wild swings in the control points between knots explain the bulging behavior—the curve passes through all the correct grid points but overshoots dramatically in between. > There's already a mitigation in place for this exact issue where closely-spaced stations cause interpolation oscillation, which was solved by equalizing station spacing along arc length. That fix applies to interior grid resampling through the axis_stations and AxisMaps system, but the docstring at line 1482 indicates the base stations are actually the boundary vertices' pinned positions, which creates a mismatch with on-surface data at exact parameters.
> 
> The real issue is that while base stations are intentionally set at boundary-vertex positions to avoid singular interpolation from quantile-based stations, this design choice means they inherit the unevenness of the original mesh tessellation itself. In this case, the outer loop has many small steps clustered near smooth curve regions, then suddenly jumps to much larger increments near the far corner—exactly the kind of uneven sampling that causes problems.

> So the `axis_stations` array at that location uses the boundary vertices' averaged UV positions as anchor points, then fills in the space between consecutive anchors with evenly-spaced subdivisions based on the target interior density. The key insight is that subdivision within each segment between two boundary vertices is uniform, but the segments themselves can vary in length across the boundary.
> 
> When one segment is much longer than its neighbors in UV or arc-length space, it still gets the same number of subdivisions `k` (computed once from the target density and total base length), so the longer segment ends up sparsely sampled relative to its actual length while shorter segments are densely packed. This creates the exact pattern we see in the outer loop — many small steps followed by one large jump — and when that sparsely-subdivided long segment gets fed into the B-spline interpolation with chordlength parametrization, the large disparity in knot spacing destabilizes the system, producing the wild control points we observed in the bug report.
> 
> The real issue is that `axis_stations` uses uniform subdivision per boundary segment based on vertex indices rather than arc-length or chord-length, so when a mesh has non-uniform vertex density (common in CAD exports with adaptive tessellation — fine facets on curves, coarse on flat areas), the resulting grid columns and rows end up with drastically different physical spacing, which can push OCCT's interpolation solver into near-singular territory.
> 
> The validation check only evaluates the fitted surface at the same discrete grid nodes used to build it, so a wildly oscillating interpolant that still passes exactly through those nodes will report near-zero residual even while bulging catastrophically between them — this is the classic Runge phenomenon, where the fit looks perfect at the sample points but oscillates wildly in between.
> 
> I've confirmed the root cause by inspecting the exported geometry directly: there's an explicit garbage control point at (9.000006, -72.516, -154.141) mm, roughly 150mm away from the 20mm cube, embedded in the boundary curve of the B-spline surface fitted to the "X" letter's engraving-profile region. This single bad control point is what creates the sliver extending far beyond the cube — the fitted surface still passes exactly through all its intended data points at the knots (which is why the residual check passes), but the control point wildness causes the bulging between them.
> 
> The issue stems from how `axis_stations()` constructs the fitting grid: it takes the region's boundary mesh vertices directly and then subdivides evenly between adjacent pairs by a constant factor. When the mesh's own boundary tessellation is highly non-uniform — fine in curved or filleted areas but coarse elsewhere — this creates poorly-conditioned interpolation points that lead to the wild control point behavior. The validation check only samples the surface at the same grid nodes used to build it, so an interpolant will naturally reproduce those points perfectly — it's blind to bulging between nodes. That's why the residuals look flawless (1e-14 mm) even though the control points are wildly off. The visible defects on the X face stem from this same underlying issue: the bulge likely causes the trimmed wire to self-intersect, triggering either OCCT's own validity check or the importer's repair logic to reject the face entirely.
> 
> On the Y face, a similarly problematic segment creates a bulge that the importer accepts and renders as visible slivers extending beyond the cube. Looking at their specific concerns: the small triangles are being assigned to surfaces correctly (all 641 mesh faces get surfaces), and the planar slivers are intentional per the design rules. The real problem isn't that boundary points are too close together — it's the ratio between very close boundary points and the larger geometry they're trying to approximate.
> 
> The core issue is uneven boundary-vertex spacing combined with chord-length interpolation that doesn't check for inter-node bulging. I should verify this same problematic pattern exists on the Y-side region and scan the full STEP file systematically to map which faces and regions have these wildly-off points, confirming whether this is a systemic issue with adaptively tessellated letter engravings across multiple regions.

```bash
        cur_id = m.group(1)
        buf = line
    else:
        buf += " " + line.strip()

def refs(s):

# find CARTESIAN_POINT wild 2D points >25

for id_, s in entities.items():
    m = re.match(r"#\d+ = CARTESIAN_POINT\('[^']*',\(([^)]*)\)\)", s)
    if m:
        vals = [float(v) for v in m.group(1).split(",")]
        if len(vals) == 2 and max(abs(v) for v in vals) > 25:
            wild2d_ids.append(id_)

print("wild 2D point count:", len(wild2d_ids))

# reverse index: point id -> list of entities referencing it (curves)

for pid in wild2d_ids:
    for id_, s in entities.items():
        if s.startswith(f"#{id_} = B_SPLINE_CURVE_WITH_KNOTS") and f"#{pid}" in s:
            point_to_curve.setdefault(pid, []).append(id_)

# now find which DEFINITIONAL_REPRESENTATION / PCURVE / SURFACE_CURVE reference each curve, to find surface

            for id2, s2 in entities.items():
                if s2.startswith(f"#{id2} = PCURVE") and f"#{id_}" in s2:

for cid in curve_ids:
```

```bash
import re
path = "/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-sethml-src-brepper/011d6a78-7c90-4697-8ebc-bbf9004cc098/scratchpad/xyz-cube.step"
raw = open(path, encoding="utf-8", errors="replace").read()
lines = raw.split("\n")
entities = {}
buf = ""
cur_id = None
for line in lines:
    m = re.match(r"#(\d+)\s*=", line)
    if m:
        if cur_id is not None:
            entities[cur_id] = buf
        cur_id = m.group(1)
        buf = line
    else:
        buf += " " + line.strip()
if cur_id is not None:
    entities[cur_id] = buf

# find all PLANE entities with origin y in {-9,-10} (Y region walls)
plane_ids = set()
for id_, s in entities.items():
    if s.startswith(f"#{id_} = PLANE"):
        m = re.match(r"#\d+ = PLANE\('[^']*',#(\d+)\)", s)
        if m:
            ax = m.group(1)
            axs = entities.get(ax, "")
            m2 = re.match(r"#\d+ = AXIS2_PLACEMENT_3D\('[^']*',#(\d+)", axs)
            if m2:
                origin_s = entities.get(m2.group(1), "")
                m3 = re.match(r"#\d+ = CARTESIAN_POINT\('[^']*',\(([^)]*)\)\)", origin_s)
                if m3:
                    vals = [float(v) for v in m3.group(1).split(",")]
                    if len(vals)==3 and abs(vals[1]+9)<1.01 or (len(vals)==3 and abs(vals[1]+10)<0.01):
                        plane_ids.add(id_)

print("Y-region plane ids:", plane_ids)

# find B_SPLINE_SURFACE_WITH_KNOTS entities and their pole point ids, find ones whose poles are mostly near y<0 (Y region)
bss = {}
for id_, s in entities.items():
    if s.startswith(f"#{id_} = B_SPLINE_SURFACE_WITH_KNOTS") or s.startswith(f"#{id_} = ADVANCED_FACE"):
        pass

for id_, s in entities.items():
    if "B_SPLINE_SURFACE_WITH_KNOTS" in s and s.startswith(f"#{id_} ="):
        pts = re.findall(r"#(\d+)", s)
        # sample first point to guess region
        first_pt = entities.get(pts[0], "")
        m = re.match(r"#\d+ = CARTESIAN_POINT\('[^']*',\(([^)]*)\)\)", first_pt)
        if m:
            vals = [float(v) for v in m.group(1).split(",")]
            bss[id_] = vals

for k,v in list(bss.items())[:20]:
    print("bspline surf", k, "first pole", v)
EOF
```

Confirmed — this is a real, severe defect, not just a validity nag. I traced it all the way into the exported STEP geometry.

## What's actually in the file

I parsed the STEP output and looked at the raw control points of the 6 fitted B‑spline surfaces. Four of them contain garbage:

| Surface | Region | Pole coordinate range | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| `#4056` | X letter (patch 1) | y −2.9..7.0, z −4.4..15.9 | mildly out of range |
| `#4629` | X letter (patch 2) | **y −72.5, z −154.1** | catastrophic |
| `#7284` | Y letter (patch 1) | x −3.3..2.8, z 4.1..15.1 | clean |
| `#7775` | Y letter (patch 2) | x −2.8..2.7, z 4.1..15.5 | clean |
| `#8618` | Y letter (patch 3) | **x 13.85, z −17.2** | severe |
| `#9102` | Y letter (patch 4) | z −6.8 | out of range |

The cube is only 20mm (±10mm in x/y, 0–20 in z). A control point at `(9.000006, −72.516, −154.141)` — I traced this to a specific edge curve shared by faces 3/49 (`stage3.rs`) — is ~150mm from anything in the model. That's your "letter almost missing" (severely distorted faces get dropped/collapsed on import) and "slivers extending far beyond the cube" (the milder overshoots on `#4056`/`#9102` are exactly the kind of stray wing a viewer renders as a spike). Notably: the Z letter used **zero** B‑spline surfaces (all-planar per the log), and both its faces are undamaged — so the defect tracks precisely with which regions went through the B‑spline fitter.

## Why it happens

Stage 2.7's fitting grid is built by [`axis_stations`](src/stage2/bspline.rs:1493). Per design (§2.7 rule 5), the "base" stations are pinned exactly at the region's boundary mesh vertices, then evenly subdivided *within each segment* between adjacent vertices up to a density target:

```rust
let k = (target.saturating_sub(1)).div_ceil(base.len() - 1).max(1);
for w in base.windows(2) {
    for step in 0..k { out.push(w[0] + (w[1] - w[0]) * step as f64 / k as f64); }
}
```

`k` is one constant for the whole axis. If the mesh's own boundary tessellation is uneven — finely faceted where the letter has a slight fillet, coarsely faceted where it's genuinely flat (matches your observation: "purely planar... maybe some slight curvature") — one segment ends up many times longer in 3D than its neighbors, but gets subdivided by the *same* `k`. That unevenness goes straight into [`PointsToBSplineSurface::Interpolate`](src/stage2/bspline.rs:1630) with chord-length parametrization. A big jump in consecutive knot spacing is exactly the condition that ill-conditions that interpolation's linear solve — OCCT reports `is_done() == true` and hands back a surface whose control polygon has spurious far-flung points compensating for the near-singularity, even though the curve/surface still passes through the real data points at the knots (interpolation guarantees that part). Between those knots, it bulges catastrophically — which is what you're seeing.

This is why it's invisible in the tool's own logs: `validate_fit`'s "residual vs own grid" check (printed as e.g. `max 6.583e-14 mm`) only samples the fitted surface *at the same discrete nodes it was built from*. An interpolant reproduces those by construction, so the check is structurally blind to inter-node bulging — a surface can log a "perfect" 1e‑14mm fit and still contain a 150mm-off control point.

## Answering your specific questions

- **Small triangles not fit into their planar surface?** No — that's not it. All 641 mesh faces get assigned a surface; nothing is orphaned or misclassified.
- **Points too close together?** Not quite "too close" — it's the opposite pairing: many *very short* boundary segments (fine tessellation on a slightly curved stretch) sitting next to one *much longer* segment (coarse tessellation on a flat stretch) in the same fitting grid. That spacing *ratio* is what ill-conditions the interpolation, not raw proximity.
- **Something else** — yes: a gap in validation. The fit-quality check only evaluates at the interpolation nodes themselves, so a wildly oscillating "successful" fit passes silently.

## Where I'd point a fix

Either constrain `axis_stations` to equalize *3D arc length* per station (like `AxisMaps`/`axis_profile` already does elsewhere in the same file, which was built to fix a related "near-coincident stations" oscillation on the saddle model — see the comment at [stage2/bspline.rs:1040](src/stage2/bspline.rs:1040)) rather than mesh-vertex-index spacing, or add a bounding-box sanity check on the fitted surface's control points (reject/retry if any pole falls far outside the region's own bounding box) so this fails loudly in Stage 2.7 instead of silently exporting garbage. I haven't made either change — want me to implement one of these, or dig further into exactly which mesh segment triggers the bad row first?

> Looking at the measurement data across these regions, the numbers tell a clear story. The "9 faces" region shows a max angle deviation of about 3.1° with a planar deviation of 0.02mm, while the "10 faces" region has tighter tolerances at 2.1° and 0.017mm respectively. The Y-axis region marked as "clean" appears to be the reference point for comparison.
> 
> What's striking is that all six regions cluster tightly around the same planarity characteristics—angular deviations between 1.5° and 3.7°, with planar deviations in the 12-25 micron range. This isn't curved geometry at all; it's genuinely flat, with the normal wobble almost certainly just STL quantization noise from the export process rather than actual surface curvature. The critical insight is that there's no correlation between the severity labels and the actual geometric measurements. directly answers the user's core questions: these regions are genuinely planar (deviations under 0.025mm, well within STL quantization noise), so NURBS is fundamentally the wrong choice for representing them — a simple plane would be both correct and numerically stable, whereas fitting a cubic B-spline to inherently flat geometry is wasteful and prone to the ill-conditioning that caused the failures.
> 
> The design rule that should have caught this exists in the plan: components planar to fitting exactness should stay planar rather than become B-spline surfaces, but the threshold for "planar to fitting exactness" is set at 10× vertex tolerance, and I'm now checking what the default vertex tolerance actually is. oo tight — the default surface tolerance of 0.4mm would easily accommodate all six regions since they're all well under 0.025mm deviation, yet the stricter planarity check forces them into the B-spline fitter anyway, which then struggles and produces worse results than a simple plane fit would have.
> 
> The real solution is to align that planarity gate in Stage 2.6 with the same surface tolerance threshold used elsewhere in the pipeline, rather than keeping it at this unnecessarily restrictive 10× vertex tolerance bound.  Doing so would correctly identify all six regions as flat planes, avoid the numerically unstable curve-fitting path, and produce cleaner STEP output with actual planar surfaces instead of high-order NURBS patches. Now I need to locate that exact threshold in the code and get the line numbers to confirm this.

## Findings

I added temporary instrumentation to dump the mesh face indices behind each of the 6 B-spline regions, plus a best-fit-plane deviation for each. Here's what came back:

| Region | Faces | Max normal-angle spread | Max deviation from best-fit plane | Fit outcome |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| X patch 1 | 9 | 3.10° | **0.0208 mm** | mild garbage |
| X patch 2 | 10 | 2.06° | **0.0173 mm** | catastrophic (150mm) |
| Y patch 1 | 10 | 3.65° | **0.0251 mm** | clean |
| Y patch 2 | 10 | 2.03° | **0.0123 mm** | clean |
| Y patch 3 | 13 | 2.20° | **0.0212 mm** | severe garbage |
| Y patch 4 | 14 | 1.50° | **0.0139 mm** | out of range |

## Are they truly non-planar?

No. All six regions are flat to within **0.012–0.025mm** of a single best-fit plane — a couple hundredths of a millimeter, well under a typical FDM layer height (0.1–0.3mm) and well under this pipeline's own `--surface-tolerance` default (0.4mm) that's used to accept *other* surface types. The 1.5°–3.7° face-normal wobble is consistent with ordinary STL export/mesh-processing noise on flat facets, not real design curvature — and it's essentially the same magnitude across all six regions, whether they ended up garbage or clean. There's no curvature signal correlating with which ones broke; the two "clean" surfaces (Y patch 1/2) are if anything *less* flat than some of the broken ones. This reads as flat, straight-walled embossed/engraved lettering — exactly what you said it looked like by eye.

## Is NURBS the right tool here?

No — and I found exactly why the pipeline chose it anyway. Stage 2.7 has a guard for precisely this case ([stage2/bspline.rs:2141-2161](src/stage2/bspline.rs:2141)):

```rust
// Nearly planar regions get a plane, not a B-spline that overfits noise.
let plane_tol = 10.0 * config.vertex_tolerance_mm;
```

`vertex_tolerance_mm` defaults to `5e-5`mm, so `plane_tol` = **0.0005mm**. All six regions measured 0.012–0.025mm — roughly **25–50× over** that gate — so every one of them fell through to the B-spline fitter instead of being accepted as a plane. The threshold is calibrated for "on-surface exactness" (matching CAD-exact meshes down near machine precision), not for real-world STL noise, so it never fires for data like this.

That's the actual root cause, one level up from what I found last time: the interpolation ill-conditioning I diagnosed before is a real bug, but it's only reachable because a region that should have been trivially accepted as a plane gets routed into the numerically fragile NURBS path instead. Fix the gate, and these regions never reach `axis_stations`/`Interpolate` at all — no ill-conditioning to trigger, no garbage control points, smaller/simpler STEP output, and the "why does this look flat but got fit with a curve" mismatch you noticed goes away at the source.

I'd size `plane_tol` off `surface_tolerance_mm` (or at least a much larger multiple of `vertex_tolerance_mm`) rather than touching the interpolation code — it fixes the actual mismatch between the model's geometry and the fitter chosen for it, rather than just hardening a fitter that shouldn't have run in the first place. Want me to make that change?

Then implement the fix, test that this file produces reasonable results, and commit.

> The new approach computes arc-length positions along the profile curve, then uses a merge pass to drop interior stations that are too close together — measuring closeness as a fraction of the median gap between original stations, with a threshold around 0.25.
> 
> After merging, the subdivision pass determines how many new stations to insert between each kept pair, balancing a global density target against local gap sizes, though I'm still working out whether to cap the per-segment ratio against the median of kept gaps or something else to handle cases where fine details coexist with coarse regions. So subdividing the flat gaps into chunks matching the median fillet size gives me around 220 total stations across the grid, which is manageable computationally. I could cap the total station count as a safety valve—if it hits something like 4× the target or 256 stations—and let the pole-bbox safety net handle any ratio violations. But now I'm wondering if I should be anchoring the subdivision target differently: maybe each gap should be capped at some ratio times the minimum gap rather than relative to the median.
> 
> If I use the minimum gap as the anchor (fillet 0.05·s), that's more aggressive—it forces chunks of 0.2s, giving 5 subdivisions per flat unit. The real concern is adjacent spans: overshoot amplification scales roughly with the square of the adjacent knot ratio, so local transitions matter most. Rather than subdividing globally, I could enforce a grading constraint where each gap is bounded by the ratio times its neighboring gaps, so gap_i ≤ R·gap_{i-1} and ≤ R·gap_{i+1}—like a mesh grading rule that keeps the spacing smooth across the boundary.
> 
> For a practical approach, I'll set a global bound as R times the minimum kept gap (with R around 6) plus a hard cap of 128 total stations. Working through the fillet-plus-flat example: if the minimum gap is 0.0045 and the flat gap is 0.0909, then bounding flats to 6 times the minimum gives 0.027, which means each flat needs about 4 subdivisions to stay within that limit.
> 
> Testing this on the xyz cube with gaps ranging from 0.175 to 3.66 mm: the minimum is 0.175, so the cap becomes 1.05 mm, forcing only the largest gap to split into 4 pieces. This keeps the ratio at 5.2, which should help, though the overshoot from local deviation still hovers around 0.5 mm in the worst case.
> 
> The earlier estimate of 441× wiggle (8–130 mm range) loosely matched what I observed. With R=6, the overshoot is 0.55 mm, which exceeds the 0.4 mm surface tolerance and might fail validation. Dropping to R=3 gives a cap of 0.525 mm, splitting the 3.66 mm gap into 7 pieces with a ratio of 3—that yields an overshoot of only 0.18 mm, which is acceptable. For the fillet example, R=3 means a cap of 0.0135 mm, creating 7 pieces per flat and about 90 total stations, staying within the 128-station limit.
> 
> Now I'm working through how the merge threshold constrains the subdivision. Setting min_gap at 0.25× median ensures that any merged gap is at least 0.75× median, which bounds the subdivision ratio to roughly 1.33n and keeps total station count under ~2.3n. The pathological case is when one tiny unmerged gap forces dense subdivision across the whole boundary, capped at 128 stations. If the median is very small relative to the boundary (many tiny gaps with one giant flat), the worst-case subdivision could explode, but the merge logic keeps it bounded. Subdivision must respect arc-length uniformity rather than UV space, since Floater's compression can make uniform UV spacing uneven in 3D. I'm creating a helper function that maps from arc position back to UV coordinates using the profile's inverse relationship, mirroring how the base stations are computed, with careful attention to round-tripping conversions.
> 
> The arc profile itself is an approximation averaged across multiple probe isolines with some identity blending, which is sufficient for conditioning. For periodic bands, the same station merging and splitting logic applies through the u_side_a and u_side_b call sites, where the closing station handling accounts for the duplicate at the seam.
> 
> Now I need to add a safety check in fit_grid after the surface is computed: I'll grab the pole positions from the B-spline surface using the pole accessor, compute a bounding box from those points, and verify that no poles fall outside an inflated margin around the grid data—if they do, something's gone wrong with the fit.
> 
> I'm also reconsidering the parametrization strategy: switching from chord-length to centripetal parametrization could help tame overshoot on uneven grids since it compresses extreme chord ratios down to more manageable parameter space differences. That's a simple change but would affect all 447 existing model fits.
> 
> The catch is that the test suite compares against exact OCCT geometry references, so any parametrization shift would cause reference mismatches even if the surfaces stay within tolerance. The station-based fix is safer since it only impacts models with problematic merging or subdivision, leaving well-tessellated models untouched.
> 
> I should validate this approach empirically — maybe write a quick 1D chord-length interpolation test with the actual station data, then run it on the xyz cube to check the poles and measure the results. Running the full test suite afterward would confirm nothing breaks.
> 
> Looking at the pattern across patches: X patch 2 had that massive 3.66mm chord that my subdivision fixes, and X patch 1 shows the same issue with a 3.7mm chord at the start. Y patches follow the same pattern too — big chords appearing at the beginning and sometimes at the end. I need to verify the station positions are being averaged correctly between sides and mapped to the arc profile along the axis direction, since the gaps I'm measuring are 3D boundary chords and arc_fraction uses the sampled 3D arc, so they should be consistent. Let me check the minimum gap when merging in xyz space.
> 
> For patch 2, the gap of 0.175 is well below the median of about 0.33, so it doesn't meet the merge threshold. Now I'm thinking through whether to add the midpoint check to validate_fit for robustness — the user wants something that handles all reasonable geometries. I can use conditioned stations as prevention, pole-bbox as catastrophe detection, and midpoint sampling for precise detection, but that adds complexity with holes and approximate paths. I'll focus on prevention and pole-bbox detection now and save the midpoint idea for later, though I should reconsider the pole-bbox threshold carefully to avoid breaking the eight existing NURBS models that currently pass.
> 
> Looking at the actual poles for legitimate fits — closed periodic bands like elliptical cylinders have poles just outside by O(r/n), which is small, and gentler shapes like bspline_hill and saddle are fine with a 50% diagonal margin. I need to place the pole check in fit_grid right after the surface is created and periodic knots are rescaled, before validate_fit runs, computing the data bbox and surface poles. If it fails, I move to the next method, which leaves the region as single-face planars if all periodic methods fail. I should check if the BSplineSurface binding has a pole accessor in opencascade-sys.
> 
> The plan is to grep for the pole accessor API and integrate it at the axis_stations call site, rewrite axis_stations with arc-space conditioning including merge and per-segment subdivide for caps, add the pole-bbox sanity check in fit_grid, then build and test on the xyz cube to verify no wild poles in the STEP output, improved step-measure-tolerance metrics, and reduced or eliminated BRepCheck warnings.
> 
> Now I need to run the full test suite (checking AGENTS.md for the exact command, likely cargo test --release or run_test_table.sh expecting around 477 passing tests in ~3 minutes), run cargo clippy for linting, and then commit following the AGENTS.md format with model name and user prompts.

```
# Instructions for LLM Coding Assistants

This file contains guidelines for AI/LLM coding assistants working on this project. Read ALL of AGENTS.md - no just the first 100 lines.

The shell is zsh. Use zsh quoting conventions. "echo ===" will fail, and globs in flags must be quoted.

### Commit Checklist (MANDATORY — verify before every commit)

- \`cargo clippy\` emits no warnings.
- Build passes with no errors or warnings.

## Project Layout

This is a Rust project. Key locations:

- \`README.md\` - Program usage. Read it.
- \`DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md\` - Development plan. Read it.
- \`Cargo.toml\` — Rust project manifest
- \`src/\` — Rust source files
  - \`bin/brepper.rs\` — the main program
  - \`bin/step_measure_tolerance.rs\` — utility to measure distances from STL to correpsonding STEP
  - \`bin/viewer.rs\` — GUI to visualize STL or STEP files
- \`scripts/\` — shell scripts for batch operations over test data
  - \`step_distance_table.sh\` - runs \`step_measure_tolerance\` over all STL/STEP pairs and summarizes statistics
- \`tests/\` — STL and STEP test file pairs
  - \`manual/\` — handcrafted simple models
  - \`onshape/\` — models exported from Onshape
  - \`ccad/generated/\` — models generated via CodeCAD

### OCCT / opencascade-rs

The project uses the \`opencascade-sys\` crate (auto-generated Rust FFI bindings to OpenCASCADE Technology) via a path dependency. MANDATORY: All useful C++ OCCT APIs should be available in the binding - if you want to use one that doesn't seem to be bound, complain loudly about it AND STOP.

\`\`\`
../opencascade-rs/crates/opencascade-sys   ← Rust FFI bindings (8700+ types)
../opencascade-rs/crates/opencascade-sys/PORTING.md  ← Naming conventions & usage patterns
../opencascade-rs/crates/opencascade-sys/generated/  ← All generated .rs modules
../opencascade-rs/crates/occt-sys/OCCT/src/  ← OCCT C++ source (for reading docs/headers)
../opencascade-rs/crates/occt-sys/OCCT/dox/  ← OCCT C++ documentation
\`\`\`

The \`builtin\` feature (default) compiles OCCT from source — first build takes ~8 minutes. Subsequent builds are fast.

## Commit Messages

All commit messages must include:

1. **A clear description** of the changes made
2. **The AI agent/model** used to generate the changes (e.g., "Claude Sonnet 4 (Anthropic, 2025)")
3. **The user prompts** that led to the changes

Format:
\`\`\`
<descriptive commit title>

<detailed description of changes>

---
Generated with assistance from <model name> (<provider>, <year>)

User prompt(s):
- "<prompt 1>"
- "<prompt 2>"
  (include context for prompts that reference previous options/decisions)
\`\`\`

## Development Plan Maintenance

Keep DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md up to date with each commit:

1. **Mark completed items** - Check off (\`[x]\`) any Implementation Phases items that are completed by the commit
2. **Add new tasks** - If work reveals new tasks or sub-tasks not in the plan, add them to the appropriate phase
3. **Modify existing tasks** - If a planned approach changes (e.g., different algorithm, new dependency), update the relevant sections
4. **Include in commit** - Changes to DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md should be part of the same commit as the implementation work

## README Maintenance

Keep README.md up to date when changes affect user-facing behavior:

1. **New dependencies** - Add to the dependencies list with installation instructions
2. **New CLI options** - Update the usage examples and options documentation
3. **Changed defaults** - Document any changes to default parameter values
4. **New features** - Add brief descriptions of significant new capabilities
5. **Include in commit** - README updates should be part of the same commit as the feature work

### IMPORTANT: Time Builds and Tests

**Always** prefix build and test commands with \`time\`. When they take more than 2 minutes ask the user whether to speed them up.

Correct: \`time make -j4 2>&1\`
Correct: \`time ctest --output-on-failure -j4 2>&1\`

## Temporary Test Code

When writing temporary code for debugging or testing:

1. **Put files in \`tmp/\`** - This directory is in the project root and ignored by git. Don't use \`/tmp\` or other directories outside the workspace.

2. **Don't delete temporary code** - Leave it in \`tmp/\` in case it's useful later.

## Library Reuse

Before implementing non-trivial functionality (text rendering, file parsing, data structures, etc.), search for existing open-source libraries that provide that functionality. Evaluate whether using a library will be better for code simplicity and maintainability than writing it from scratch. Consider factors like: compatibility with existing dependencies, maintenance status, API complexity, and how well the library fits the use case. Then go with your decision — either use the library or implement it yourself — and briefly explain why.

## Generating CodeCAD Test Models

Test models can be generated with ccad - see \`../codecad/docs\` for documentation. To regenerate the STL and STEP files for CodeCAD-based tests:

\`\`\`bash
# Requires 'ccad' tool installed (https://codecad.xyz)
./tests/ccad/generate_models.sh
\`\`\`
```

```
1404	/// clamped fitted hypothesis of a seam-cut band into two halves along the

1406	/// Each half spans only part of the surface's parameter range, so its two
1407	/// cut edges are geometrically distinct and no periodic-seam machinery is
1408	/// needed — but those two edges are reconstruction artifacts a reference
1409	/// model (one periodic face with a real seam) does not have, so the
1410	/// stage 3.1 edge compare will flag them.
1411	fn split_cut_hypothesis(

1428	    }

1430	    let mut faces_b = Vec::new();
1431	    for (rf, &fi) in region.faces.iter().enumerate() {
```

> 
> The interpolation algorithm amplifies small mesh noise through the knot vector's inherited spacing ratio — a C2 cubic's control points can swing wildly when adjacent knot intervals differ this drastically, and the excursions happen between data points where validation routines never catch them.
> 
> My fix has two parts: first, I'll reparametrize the station spacing using arc fractions from the AxisMaps profile to create a well-conditioned grid, then merge any stations that cluster too close together to avoid near-coincident knots.

> The approach is to iterate to fixpoint: repeatedly scan through gaps and split any that violate the adjacent ratio limit R by comparing each gap to its immediate neighbors, using their original sizes to avoid cascading effects from earlier splits in the same pass. An iterate-to-fixpoint approach with a bounded loop (say 8 iterations) would handle transitive violations cleanly — each pass splits any gap exceeding R times its neighbors' minimum, and the worst offenders get halved each iteration, converging quickly. I'd also cap total stations at 256 as a safety guard. But now I'm wondering if this adjacent-ratio constraint is even necessary, or if something simpler like "no gap exceeds RATIO_CAP times the median" would be robust enough across different input geometries.

<!-- msg_011Cd5C5vPZ32cja7W1boCSp -->

I have the full picture now. Let me reason through the fix design before writing code.

**Root cause, precisely:** `axis_stations` pins grid stations at boundary-vertex positions, and this boundary mixes fine facets (0.175mm chords, on the slightly-curved stretches) with one long straight chord (3.66mm) — a 21:1 spacing jump fed directly into chord-length-parametrized C2 cubic interpolation. A C2 interpolant's overshoot at a knot-spacing discontinuity grows roughly with the *square* of the adjacent-gap ratio (21² ≈ 440× amplification of the mesh's ~0.02–0.3mm flatness noise ≈ 10–130mm — matching the observed 150mm poles). The existing `k`-subdivision divides *every* segment equally, so it can never fix a ratio. And validation only samples at/near data points, so inter-node spikes ship silently.

**The fix, two independent layers:**
1. **Prevention** — condition the station spacing in `axis_stations`, in 3D-arc measure (the `AxisMaps` profile already provides exactly this): merge near-coincident stations (the known "singular interpolation" failure), and subdivide long segments until no gap exceeds ~2× its smaller neighbor (graded spacing, like mesh grading). Subdivision goes uniform-in-arc, not uniform-in-UV. Boundary vertices keep their exact pinned positions (the arch rule stays intact — and mid-segment stations on a *long* segment are safe by construction: a long boundary chord only exists where the mesh is flat).
2. **Detection** — a pole sanity check in `fit_grid`: a B-spline surface lies in the convex hull of its control points, so any pole far outside the data grid's bounding box proves the surface swings far off the mesh. Reject such fits (→ retry ladder / planar fallback) instead of shipping them. This makes the failure mode impossible to ship silently for *any* input.

I deliberately avoid switching to centripetal parametrization (would perturb every model's fit globally); graded stations achieve uniformity directly with zero effect on well-tessellated models.
